mr president, the contribution of cars to total air pollution is 60% for carbon monoxide, 30% for nitrogen oxides, 50% for hydrocarbons and 3.5% for sulphur dioxide. given that exhaust gases are emitted at the height of human breathing, cars rank among the biggest polluters of urban air, with a share of 80 to 99%. in order to meet the commission's requirements for the emission standards to be met by new vehicles, car manufacturers do not hesitate to present misleading information, as was the case with the tampered software in the volkswagen scandal in september 2015. but they don't stop there. in the new scandal that has broken out in the german automobile industry, volkswagen, daimler and bmw, wanting to prove that engines are not dangerous, have used not only monkeys but also humans in their experiments to see whether diesel exhaust gases are harmful to primate mammals. it's not enough that volkswagen and the institute for respiratory research apologized after this incredible revelation. we demand exemplary punishment for all involved in this scandal.

member of the commission. – madam president, terrorists need to raise and move funds to maintain their networks, cover their logistical needs and commit their attacks. we need to close down the space in which they can do this. countering terrorist financing is not only about limiting their capacity to act, but also about, as we have just heard, using financial intelligence to detect and stop terrorists before they act. recent attacks perpetrated in europe have been financed with relatively small sums of money, and that poses some new challenges to those who are working on the front line to prevent terrorist attacks. we need to give law enforcement and our public authorities the tools they need to do their job and to help keep us safe. so i very much welcome the timely recommendations in this report. tackling terrorist financing is a high priority for all of us. from the commission side, it is a key part of our work to build an effective security union. our approach was set out in the action plan for strengthening the fight against terrorist financing of february 2016, and we are now taking that work forward on two fronts: detecting and preventing terrorist funding, and disrupting the sources of revenue. that covers a range of different measures: preventing the misuse of the financial system for money laundering and terrorist financing; increasing cooperation and the exchange of information between competent authorities, in particular financial intelligence units; tracing the financial movements of terrorists; improving the effectiveness of asset-freezing systems; reinforcing the judicial response to terrorist financing and money laundering; supporting third countries in countering terrorist financing and money laundering in their implementation of the financial action task force standards; supporting customs authorities with tools to identify sources of revenue of terrorist organisations; and supporting third countries as they engage with international partners, not only through the fatf, as i mentioned, but also other bodies such as the anti-isis coalition working group on countering financing of terrorism, and indeed the work that is done in the framework of the g-7. so i am glad to see that many of the report’s recommendations are in line with the work that we are undertaking. we welcome the emphasis in the report on information-sharing, in particular among financial intelligence units, as well as other national agencies. we welcome your support for the establishment of central bank account registries. i am glad to say, as announced in the commission work programme for this year, we will soon present initiatives to improve cross-border access to financial data for law enforcement authorities. this will include measures to facilitate access to national centralised bank account registries and retrieval systems within member states. we will also propose measures to improve the cooperation between law enforcement authorities and the various financial intelligence units. in line with the report’s recommendations, we have reinforced europol’s role in this fight against terrorist financing. we take note of the call for increasing monitoring of prepaid cards. the fifth anti-money laundering directive is a step forward, i think, in reinforcing the transparency around this means of payment. with its list of non-cooperative jurisdictions, the eu is also acting to increase the exchange of information on tax matters with third countries, as you have been discussing. this will help national administrations to detect the presence of illicit money and money-laundering. we appreciate your support for the eu-us terrorist finance tracking programme and we take note of your call for a european system to complement that. on asset-freezing, we appreciate the report’s support for existing measures. the eu’s asset-freezing efforts have been reinforced with the autonomous eu regime against al-qaeda and daesh. we appreciate the emphasis on hawala. in the supranational risk assessment of money laundering and terrorism financing risks, the commission recommended that hawala service providers should apply the requirements related to money-transfer systems under the payment services directive and the anti-money laundering directive, and we are committed to keeping this particular sector under close review. in relation to high-value goods, i would like to underline that the proposal to amend the cash control regulation expands the definition of cash to gold and other high-value commodities. we appreciate your support for our proposed regulation on the import of cultural goods. this issue remains of high importance in addressing the problem of cultural goods being illegally taken from third countries, from their proper historical and archeological context, and being brought to the eu. this can in turn foster organised crime, terrorist financing, money laundering and tax evasion. member states should also soon adopt the commission’s proposals to strengthen the vat system and reinforce the collective fight against vat fraud, which again can feed criminal and terrorist organisations. so in a nutshell, i am glad that the work the commission is doing in this area is in line with many of the measures you call for and support in today’s report. some other new measures you suggest merit further analysis. we will take a careful look at them and we will come back to you on them. countering financing of terrorism is a core component of the eu strategy in the fight against terrorism and hence in our work to build an effective security union. as the terrorists and their supporters constantly modify and change the ways they collect, move and get access to funds, we need to be ready to adopt and adapt our response. the eu needs to adapt its instruments and measures to deprive terrorists of the space and the means they use as they seek to do us harm. i count on your continued support as we take this work forward.

mr president, the european union has repeatedly used figurative and somewhat abstract terms to describe things that are actually simple and clear: the union is a set of administrative bodies. it cannot therefore have its own resources, because it does not produce anything or offer services for a fee. what is described in the reports as union resources is nothing but revenue deducted from the national budgets or collected from the citizens of the member states. vat, for example, if it did not go to the union, or the states would have more revenue, or if they deducted the amount going to the union altogether, citizens would have a significantly lower burden. moreover, whether the benefits of the union are commensurate with the costs borne by both states and citizens is a question that is not addressed in these reports and, more importantly, has never been asked of citizens themselves. after all, are the benefits such as to justify the maintenance of a huge bureaucratic apparatus? could we have the same benefits at a much lower cost with much smaller instruments simply coordinating between states and using the existing parallel state agencies?
the budget of the union, mr president, is the expression of an outdated and counterproductive system which cannot be corrected by piecemeal interventions or by dealing only with the symptoms of distortions and not with their causes. it is utopian to think that the effects of the financial, economic and social crisis are now a thing of the past, and it is unacceptable to target political movements and political opponents through the budget. the budgetary objective is not merely a formal allocation of appropriations but remains purely theoretical. it does not lead to sustainable growth, does not adequately address existing challenges or emerging threats and exacerbates economic disparities between stronger and weaker member states. unfortunately, in a united europe, where member states are dictating their economic and fiscal policies and have now lost their full national sovereignty, the well-being of citizens is entrusted to third parties without guarantees of the sound, impartial and independent management of the resources available.

mr president, as i mentioned in the debate, the troika in greece has imposed a harsh tax evasion policy through the memoranda with a particularly high vat. in particular, it has introduced legislation which abolished the preferential reduced value added tax scheme in force in the aegean islands. the low value added tax has helped the economic development of the islands due to their distance from mainland greece and also due to competition from turkey, which has a particularly low vat rate. on the basis of the current situation, lesvos, chios, samos, ikaria and leros will also lose the right to reduced value added tax from 1 july 2018. these are islands affected by huge migratory flows, so the reduced vat rate must be maintained in lesvos, chios, samos, ikaria and leros, while the reduced vat rate must be reintroduced for the aegean islands.
the european semester, mr president, is a way of reducing the sovereignty of the member states. in essence, it is a tool for controlling the economic policies of the member states and at the same time a tool for enforcing the stability pact. the member states are supposed to have full powers in employment and social policy. this is also provided for by the principle of conferral of powers laid down in the treaty and by the principle of subsidiarity. at the same time, therefore, the european union's intervention does not in fact introduce a european minimum wage, a european minimum income or a minimum unemployment benefit, and the regulations it imposes with the european semester do not tackle unemployment, reduce social exclusion or achieve social justice. they keep the precarious jobs. it's a failed policy.
mr president, at the beginning of the 21st century, cyberattacks were added to the threats that had existed in various countries up to that time. although there is no universally accepted definition of cyberspace, it can be said to be made up of the entire computer, network, internet, facilities and telecommunications capacity of a state. the threats that may arise in cyberspace are attacks aimed at destroying or disrupting the operation of the above-mentioned means. because the potential of the internet is so vast, many countries view cyber warfare as an existential threat and have therefore included it in their strategic priorities. in order to meet these requirements, states must ensure that cyber defence services cooperate with each other, exchange information and, above all, carry out exercises in order to gain the necessary experience to deal with the problem.
mr president, the fight against cybercrime is an immediate necessity for the consolidation of democracy, the free movement of ideas, the protection of personal data and respect for personality. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of personal data. a specific form of cybercrime is online interference in the context of an election campaign in several member states. this is how, among other things, fake profiles are created on facebook, in order to subsequently carry out cyberattacks with purely political aims and objectives. in this context, coordinated cyber attacks are being carried out with the aim of politically and morally discrediting all those whose political action disturbs the political and economic establishment. in this way, in addition to the criminal offences that are taking place, every concept of an open and democratic society is being violated, and all this, mr president, in view of the forthcoming european elections.
in writing. cybercrime is undoubtedly a major threat to today's world and it is going to concern our societies in the future. statistics show that cybercrime is increasing in intensity, complexity and size, with reported cybercrime exceeding traditional crime in some member states. in addition, around 80% of businesses in europe have experienced at least one cybersecurity breach. in a landmark example, just last may, the global wannacry cyberattack affected thousands of networks, computers, hospitals and rail lines across europe. these recent events have clearly demonstrated the eu's extremely high degree of vulnerability. the question therefore is how can we effectively tackle cybercrime in the context of cooperation at european level and how can we better protect european citizens? as rapporteur for the european parliament's report on combating cybercrime, i have tried to answer these questions and to deal with this complex legal and social issue in an effective way. the fight against cybercrime is not a simple matter, it requires high skills, continuous training and education, better coordination, cooperation with third countries and legal certainty.

reform should raise questions, debate ideas, but we are at the moment of political choices. it is time for europe to be ambitious in its tax reform to attract businesses, to remain globally competitive, to fight tax fraud and tax evasion and with accis we have precisely a unique answer to the concerns of both businesses and citizens and that is why this reform is so important. we are offering businesses a genuine single market with simplified, uniform rules and fair and equal conditions of competition, and we are showing citizens our determination to fight tax evasion, which is fuelled precisely by the asymmetries between national tax systems. thanks to the proposals i will make next week with your support in the future, taxation of digital businesses will also - if member states decide so - be an integral part of the accis project and so we will really have this tax architecture on corporate taxation for the 21st century that will cover the whole economy, regardless of its strata, sectors or forms. in the face of national divisions, in the face of the current administrative burdens, i believe that we have here the keystone of the modern, competitive, transparent tax system we want. i am counting on the two forthcoming presidencies, the bulgarian presidency at the moment and the austrian presidency, to complete the work. i would also like to respond to some of the points raised by members of parliament. first of all, no, this project is not an attack on the sovereignty of the member states. it is never about that in the projects we are formulating because the european commission, as you know, has no competence whatsoever, for example, on rates. it is simply a question of harmonising the conditions of operation in the single market for the benefit of businesses, and this is not just a matter for the national level, but, of course, the national level retains most of its prerogatives. of course, it is also a matter of combating tax evasion, which is facilitated by the complexities of tax borders, but here again the answer is european. ms joly returned to the use of article 116 to deal with distortions of competition in the internal market. i do not think that it can be used as a legal basis for a legislative proposal like accis, but you know that the european commission is thinking about how to return to this question of unanimity in tax matters, which we find is indeed at the very least a source of complexity, especially when we have such ambitious projects. finally, on the question of whether there is a basis for a future own resource or on the question of tax rates or tax harmonization, i repeat: by creating a common base, we are undoubtedly moving towards fiscal convergence. tax harmonization, which is an ambition i share, also depends in this context on the will of the member states, because there is no going back on tax sovereignty. i do not think there is an additional tax burden here - at any rate it is not included in the proposal - and so i think that, with all the sensitivities that are expressed here, you can move with confidence towards the common consolidated corporate tax base which is compatible with much of the views i have heard here. moreover, the rapporteurs do not seem to me to be necessarily from the same political families, and yet they have done extremely close work, since we have here a proposal which can really bring together because it is a question of modernising, of making our tax systems transparent, of combating aggressive tax optimisation. this extremely powerful tool is in your hands and as usual i believe that parliament and the commission have done a good job and that by your vote tomorrow you will show that you are standing with us and asking the member states to move forward with the same strength and the same ambition.
in writing. tax dumping and aggressive tax planning are a daily occurrence in the european union. the netherlands of dijsselbloem, luxembourg of juncker and malta are countries which provide tax avoidance incentives to the various multinationals. so aggressive tax planning is essentially the second nature of the european union, since the eu itself wants to create grey areas through unfair tax competition between member states. it is time at european level to create a fair tax system with a common corporate tax base, without grey areas, to stop the tax dumping of multinational companies and tax their profits in the countries where they are made. in addition, all the legislative 'windows' that legitimize the practice of intra-family transactions and lead to tax evasion that deprive state budgets of huge tax revenues should be closed. but there is no need for hypocrisy, since the ecofin council unanimously removed panama from the blacklist of tax havens. the key to tackling tax evasion and avoidance in the eu is therefore in the council, where important decisions have to be taken. but there are member states which not only do not want to see concrete measures taken but are completely blocking the necessary decisions.

mr president, the issue of global warming and, more generally, all the environmental challenges we face are extremely important problems. our firm and uncompromising position is that we support any initiative, move or agreement aimed at protecting the environment. the paris agreement is a step in the right direction, even if we disagree on some of its individual elements. it is extremely positive that the total area of land in europe covered by forests has increased considerably, but certain member states are facing problems. for example, due to global warming and the ongoing desertification of large parts of north africa, there has been a sharp increase in recent years in the occurrence of dust clouds from the sahara in greece, which cause problems for the health of citizens, as concentrations of dangerous heavy metals such as lead, zinc and arsenic have been detected in them. there is also a dramatic increase in forest fires due to the rise in temperature, which in turn contribute to the destruction of large areas of forest and crops. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's agricultural policy is not disproportionately restricted to the farmers of europe, because the only thing we can do is to reduce european agricultural production while at the same time importing agricultural products at a lower price from countries with much lower environmental standards. i believe that there should be serious restrictions on imports of agricultural products from countries outside the union, as only in this way will we be able to protect the environment by requiring these countries to adopt equally strict environmental standards.

the report on subsidiarity and proportionality is particularly important, madam president. we would point out that, in accordance with the principle of subsidiarity, decisions are taken as close as possible to the citizen. it is therefore a democratic principle, a principle which leads to the decentralisation of powers, and this means that the levels at which decisions are taken are chosen when it comes to matters falling within what we call the concurrent powers of the european union. it is therefore a mechanism which effectively legitimises the european project itself, since it also gives a very important role to the national parliaments, which retain a right of veto, a right of yellow card, to question the ability of brussels to decide everything. i therefore believe that if the principle of subsidiarity is properly applied, democracy will be firmly established within the framework of the european union.

member of the commission. – mr president, i would like to congratulate the rapporteur, ms scott cato, for taking the initiative to present this report. i welcome the support that the report gives to the work on sustainable finance. i also welcome the large consensus among political groups on the best way forward. this is encouraging in view of our future work and discussions on the legislative proposals on sustainable finance that the commission presented last week. these proposals already address some of your concerns. first, we will start the process of designing an eu classification system for sustainable economic activities which can inform future eu standards and labels for green financial products such as green bonds or investment funds. we are looking forward to the european parliament support on this important proposal. second, for those who manage investments on behalf of others, we propose to clarify that their duty towards their clients includes the systemic incorporation of sustainability risks into their investment processes. they will also have to disclose how they take this risk into account and what impact they expect these risks to have on returns. in addition, for investment products that actively pursue sustainable objectives, the way these objectives are incorporated into investment decisions would also need to be disclosed. third, we propose to create a new category of benchmarks consisting of two specific types of benchmarks: low carbon benchmarks and positive carbon impact benchmarks. we also propose to establish minimum standards for the methodology of these benchmarks. this will increase transparency towards investors on how these benchmarks select and weigh their underlying assets and measure their carbon footprint. in addition, providers of low carbon benchmarks will have to disclose how their methodology takes into account the esg factors. finally, we propose that investment firms and insurance distributors consider the sustainability preferences of retail investors when giving investment advice. in the coming weeks we will establish a technical expert group (we’re going to call it teg) that will further work on developing an eu classification for sustainable activities or taxonomy standards for green bonds and sustainable matrix. these are the first steps towards a more sustainable financial system. there is still some work ahead to address other concerns stated in your own initiative report. for instance, the commission is evaluating the mandatory reporting requirements to make sure they provided the right information to market participants, enabling them to assess companies’ long-term value creation and their sustainability-risk management. of course, further clarification of the role of european supervisory authorities in this process is really important. let me conclude by stressing again my appreciation of the work of the european parliament in this field. now we count on you to move as fast as possible in the coming months to adopt these measures that will encourage sustainable and green investments in europe.
the commission's proposal is to be submitted to the council for adoption. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the european parliament is able to exercise its right to vote. at the moment, there is a provision in the memorandum that says that if a court decision is issued annulling the budgetary measures imposed by the memorandum, then the government must proceed to take new budgetary measures of equivalent effect. in other words, the troika itself is thus enforcing - through memoranda - non-compliance with the decisions of the greek judiciary. secondly, the recent resignation of the president of the council of state in greece is a serious indication that the institution of judicial independence in greece is now being shaken. the report must examine how the troika operates and how it essentially destroys any notion of judicial independence in the member states below the troika.

member of the commission. – madam president, thank you for your contributions to this debate. i would like to address two specific issues that were raised in the debate. let me start with the problems of theatre lighting, which has been raised by many members: mr palmer, ms reid, mr gericke, mr marias, amongst others. i would say that for two years the commission has been reviewing the ecodesign legislation for lighting. this also covers the application of exemptions, as well as the existence of suitable replacements available on the market. in december 2017, revision proposals were discussed in the ecodesign consultation forum, consisting of representatives from member states, industry, civil society and other partners. the commission is receiving and analysing comments, including those we received in the context of the public consultation, which ran until 7 may this year. the commission also met with representatives from the theatre and entertainment sector to discuss their concerns. the outcome of those consultations will inform the further development of the ecodesign requirements, including whether a specific exemption for theatre lighting continues to be necessary. ms reid, mr telička and mr kelly also raised the issue of ict products. i would say that in the ecodesign working plan 2016-2019 the commission announced a more in-depth assessment of several ict products with a view to their possible inclusion in the working plan, including gateways, base stations and mobile smart phones. the main reason for a specific assessment of ict products is that it has proven very difficult to make a reliable estimate of their energy savings potential, given the uncertainty about future market developments. moreover, for fast-moving product sectors such as mobile phones, questions have arisen as to the suitability of the ecodesign process for establishing minimum energy and resource efficiency criteria, because, as this process takes on average around four years, the risk is that requirements would be updated by the time they enter into force. the commission has started this assessment, for example with a study that looks specifically at the material efficiency aspect of smart phones. based on the outcomes of this assessment, the commission will determine the best policy approach for improving the energy efficiency and wider circular economy aspects of mobile phones and other ict products. i will conclude by saying that i look forward to continue working with the european parliament to improve the implementation of this policy framework.

member of the commission. – mr president, addressing precarious work remains high on the commission’s agenda. i referred to the debate which took place in july 2017 when parliament adopted its resolution on working conditions and precarious employment. the commission has provided comprehensive answers to your resolution and has also proposed new initiatives. our priority now is to ensure that the prosperity generated by the recovering european economy benefits society fairly. we made huge efforts and the economy is growing again. but we are not done yet; we must ensure that no one is left behind. this is the essence of the european pillar of social rights, jointly proclaimed in november last year. today’s debate is essentially about our ability to bring the pillar agenda forward together – in the commission, parliament and the council. on the commission side, in recent years we have set milestones in the social area. before the end of this mandate, we must show eu citizens, in a very tangible way, our determination to make the union fairer and more inclusive. we should not ignore the fact that precarious work has a toll, not only on individuals but on our societies and economies as a whole. the union has developed and extended its response to this issue over the years. it has done so through specific eu directives on non-standard forms of work and through enhanced cooperation and coordination with the member states. this cooperation includes the european platform on undeclared work. this year we issued country-specific recommendations in the european semester on tackling the informal and shadow economy for bulgaria and italy. the european social fund provides financial support for disadvantaged youth or the long-term unemployed. let me assure you that the commission is strongly committed to ensuring compliance across the eu. where necessary, we initiate infringement proceedings, which may ultimately lead the commission to refer the matter to the court. the work undertaken by the committee on petitions strongly supports and influences our efforts to ensure effective action is undertaken. thank you so much for this support. we took concrete actions and put forward a comprehensive set of proposals: firstly, for a directive on predictable and transparent working conditions; secondly, for a council recommendation on access to social protection for workers and the self-employed; and thirdly, for a regulation establishing a european labour authority. through these initiatives, we want to prevent eu workers from being subjected to unfair and precarious working conditions by guaranteeing basic standards for all workers, including workers subject to particularly flexible forms of employment, such as on-demand work or zero-hour contracts; by ensuring social protection coverage regardless of the employment status; and by supporting administrative cooperation and better information to enforce existing rules. all workers in non-standard forms of employment must benefit from these basic standards. current eu rules often leave their scope to be determined by national law, which has left out too many workers from protection. it is essential for our initiatives on working conditions that all workers are covered. this legislative work must be pursued with utmost celerity so that we make these further steps towards fairness and inclusiveness in employment together before the elections.

in writing. the istanbul convention, which has not yet been ratified by all member states, takes a comprehensive and targeted approach to tackling gender-based violence. the eu's accession to the convention ensures the link between national and european law and strengthens the union's role in international fora. however, member states are not exempted from the obligation to incorporate the convention into their national law. despite the progress made, the road to eliminating gender discrimination is long. the evidence is clear: one third of all women in europe have experienced physical or sexual violence at least once in their adult lives, 20% have experienced online harassment, one in 20 women has been a victim of rape and more than one in 10 have experienced physical violence. the main factors that contribute to the occurrence of violence against women are fear of social exclusion and lack of education. effective response to all forms of gender-based violence requires coordinated prevention, information and awareness-raising actions, as well as encouraging victims to report any act of violence, away from fear, shame, social stereotypes and financial or emotional dependence on perpetrators.

mr president, almost half the land area of the european union is used for agricultural activities and that is why it is particularly important to have both statistical data collection and, above all, research on agricultural holdings. the information gathered will make it possible to identify the needs and problems facing farmers and to introduce appropriate reforms to the common agricultural policy to deal with these problems. but to be truly successful, two factors must be taken into account. the first is the frequency with which these recordings are made. the fact that the decade is between two records does not guarantee that we will be particularly successful in this endeavour. a second element to be taken into account is the specificities faced by each region of the european union. the needs of the european south, and especially greece, are different from those of the north.

mr president, the statements made by european officials after the last eurogroup meeting present an exaggerated picture of the greek economy, in order to create the appearance of success of the memorandum recipes of the last eight years. the reality, however, is that while the greek state has stopped running deficits, gross domestic product has remained stagnant over the last five years, after a frightening decline of almost 25% in the previous five years, from 2008 to 2013. the absence of growth means that the ratio of public debt to gdp cannot be improved, and the problem of over-indebtedness is not solved, but merely postponed to the future, with successive extensions. if the creditors want greece to get on its feet, they must reduce their long-term primary surplus requirements to a level no higher than 1% of gdp per year, so as to stop the growth-killing overtaxation.

mr president, the structural reform support programme has a history and has been in existence in greece since 2015, as my country is supported in the design and implementation of structural reforms. but it must be stressed that, as this programme is voluntary, we can see three conditions which will make the programme more friendly and effective. the first is that the proposed reforms should be development-oriented, i.e. focusing on removing obstacles such as bureaucracy and the unfavourable institutional framework, but also facilitating innovative financial instruments. the second is to make the word 'reforms' more creative and less imposing. for example, in education, in public health, we need changes that will give opportunities and good services to citizens so that citizens will embrace the reforms. the third is to remember that one suit doesn't fit everyone. the commission has already taken a number of initiatives in this area, including the establishment of a european network of national experts in the field of health and safety at work. in this way the reforms will be effective and the money of european taxpayers leaving cohesion will be put to good use.

mr president, i will counter what my colleague said earlier. i must acknowledge the author of this report for admitting that the steady negative growth rates of recent years have not been countered by the three memoranda imposed on greece by the creditors. usually, from previous governments, from the current government and from the institutions, we hear just the opposite. on the other hand, statistics are the easiest way to lie. eurostat tells us that unemployment in greece has fallen to about 20%. what it does not tell us is that the new jobs are usually parttime and with much lower wages than those that existed before the crisis. it is, of course, unreasonable to expect growth to accelerate when wages have fallen to current levels and when, consequently, the purchasing power of the greeks has been wiped out. indeed, the amounts granted for public investment in greece were substantial. but the bulk of this money did not remain in the country but went to the big construction companies, many of which are based in the so-called big countries of europe. it would be much more beneficial for the greeks if money were given that would end up in the real economy and help to create an economic environment that would be conducive to growth.

member of the commission. – mr president, on behalf of the commission i would like to express once more my condolences to the families and loved ones of the victims of the recent forest fires in greece. i would like also to pay tribute to the courage and the outstanding work of the brave firefighters who fought the blaze. commissioner stylianides flew to greece in the hours following the tragedy and helped in coordinating the eu response. the union’s civil protection mechanism reacted immediately to greece’s request for assistance. capacities were mobilised and deployed quickly in the affected areas. the response was substantial: first, ground forest-firefighting modules and vehicles from cyprus; second, canadair firefighting planes from italy and spain and two planes from romania were deployed; and nine member states in total made offers. helping countries that face forest fires was a top priority this summer for the european union and it remains a top priority. the commission’s emergency response coordination centre is working 24 hours a day and is in constant contact with national authorities. we stand ready to help greece further whenever needed. with regard to the wildfires in attica, as commissioner for regional policy i was in direct contact with the greek authorities from the moment we knew about the fires. i informed the greek authorities that the commission was ready to act swiftly to release the first payment as soon as possible. in addition, i highlighted the possibility, should the conditions for assistance from the european union solidarity fund be met, for greece to benefit from 95% eu co-financing towards reconstruction work supported by cohesion policy. lastly, i expressed my readiness, after the request from the greek authorities, to modify greek cohesion policy programmes to redirect money towards the regeneration of economic activity in the affected areas.

colleagues here in the european parliament that sensitivity should not be a la carte and that in greece there is neither repression nor violence. on the contrary, in the course of these three years, the social, political and democratic rights of citizens have been enhanced by a series of legislative interventions. i would like to conclude without offending your other colleague and fellow citizen, mr curcio, because he has asked specific questions. ladies and gentlemen, you all know that greece, having left the adjustment programmes, is now a country like all the other countries of the euro zone, with a peculiarity, like of course all the countries which have been in programmes of enhanced monitoring and cooperation with the institutions and particularly with the commission. like the other eurozone countries, therefore, greece will henceforth be obliged to meet the targets we have jointly set, our obligations, namely the fiscal target of a surplus of 3.5% of gdp by 2022. that's our deal. but it will be the greek government of the day that will choose the means and policies to meet these objectives, as is the case in all other eurozone countries and those that have left programmes. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. we will do the same this year in relation to 2019 and together we will take those decisions that will be the right ones in order to strengthen the growth potential of the greek economy. and growth potential means no more austerity, no more cuts, increased consumption, to the extent, of course, that this will allow us to meet the budgetary targets. it's that simple. i would like to conclude with a comment on the future of europe. the question was raised as to how we dream of the democratization of the euro area and the course of the european union from now on. we do believe in the need for political engagement. and we do believe in strengthening the role of parliament. you will remember that i was one of those who tried and succeeded in ensuring that the debate on the budgetary adjustment programme in greece over the last three years was not held behind closed doors and that the european parliament played a substantial role in that debate; that policies were not decided behind closed doors. i don't know if we've got all that. but we have made an effort in that direction. i also believe that in the crucial period ahead we must decide on a strengthened community budget, on a budgetary programme which will make it possible to strengthen convergence between the member states of the euro area and, of course, on the possibility of greater transparency and greater accountability in the period ahead. i support the view of a eurozone finance minister, but i also believe in the need for a eurozone social cohesion minister. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. we are not, and i am not, in favour of a two-speed europe. we are in favour of a functioning europe, of the enhanced cooperation provided for in the existing european treaties, especially if it is through this that our objective of a democratic, social and ecological europe can be achieved. with these thoughts, i would like to thank you all very warmly for your interest, for your questions, for your debate and to tell you that, despite our disagreements, our strong disagreements, despite our tensions, we have managed to take a step forward. we have managed to escape the great vortex of crisis. let us draw our conclusions on the causes that led us to this, so that we can now make the major changes that europe needs, so that we do not repeat the same mistakes, so that we do not find ourselves in the same difficult position as we did in previous years.

(the following is a transcript of the interpretation of the original speech from arabic into french) mr president of the european parliament, mr antonio tajani, ladies and gentlemen, i am pleased to be here today at the european parliament's headquarters in strasbourg, facing the elected representatives of 28 states who have chosen to make the european continent a model without equal in the world. a model of exchange, integration and unity at all levels, which transforms the countries of the european union into a single space in which the dreams of the peoples are merged and in which the principles and ideals are shared, while safeguarding each peculiarity and civilization and the belonging of the peoples to their land, their language, their heritage and their homeland. the strength of this democratic construction and edifice has helped to consolidate genuine peace within the european continent, thus putting an end to the temptation to resort to war as the only method of solving problems. these peoples and states believe that their strength, interests and prosperity depend on their unity and shared democratic values, not on conflict to ensure their success. it is very regrettable that we are currently witnessing the emergence of certain separatist and secessionist tendencies which threaten the balance of this pioneering building in the meeting of peoples. ladies and gentlemen, at this time in our history when the voice of humanity is weakening and the interests of the great powers are overstepping all bounds to the detriment of justice and human solidarity, we believe that only dialogue, mutual respect and a return to the implementation of the universal principles of human rights, of communities and of countries will help to defuse the violence, wars and extremism which have turned the world and our region of the middle east into a place inflamed by hatred, fear and tension. war, although the economy is the real engine, cannot be built on a solid foundation; it requires a cracked, permeable ground ready to burn. nothing like the incitement to intolerance, religious, communal and ethnic extremism, nothing like the rejection of the other to cause this disintegration, to crack the foundation and ignite the flames of war. what is happening in our region today ties us even more closely to the very foundations on which the lebanese state was built and, above all, to the democratic regime that characterises it, which - despite certain shortcomings - brings together all the components of the lebanese people and reflects the very essence of the message of lebanon, a country of coexistence, diversity, pluralism and cultural richness. the conflict in our region has divided lebanon politically, but not nationally. the political speeches were heated, but they did not set the powder on fire. when some left the normal path to embrace extremism and fundamentalist thought, they were immediately rejected by both their community and the country. indeed, lebanese society, by its very nature, is not a fertile ground for extremism and the rejection of others. respect for freedom of belief, freedom of expression and opinion and the right to differ are an integral part of lebanese culture. this lebanese characteristic helps the country to overcome its problems and consolidate its peace and stability. it can also serve as an example for other countries to build a future that is more in tune and in step with modernity and human rights, especially if we succeed in closing some gaps and achieving the democratic maturity we aspire to. in this context, lebanon greatly appreciates the european union's awareness of its specificity, its uniqueness. the parliamentary assembly of the council of europe expressed this in the second paragraph of its resolution 2150, in which it stresses the many specificities and the great religious diversity that make lebanon a unique country. lebanon is the oldest democracy in the middle east, political power is shared equally between christians and muslims, making it an example of peaceful coexistence which must be supported if it is to continue along this path. ladies and gentlemen, dear members of parliament, last may lebanon held parliamentary elections, despite some political tensions which delayed their holding, and they were held under a modern electoral law based on proportional representation. these elections resulted in a more balanced and precise representation of the political forces present, thus allowing for greater diversity within the parliament and thus strengthening democratic life. in this context, i must therefore welcome the work carried out by the eu election observation mission, led by mrs elena valenciano, which accompanied the various stages of the preparation of the elections and the conduct of the voting. this election observation mission of the european parliament presented a positive report on the electoral process last july. the report contains a number of new recommendations designed to fill in some of the gaps. these recommendations will certainly be taken into account in the future. we wish to strengthen cooperation between our parliament and the european parliament by intensifying joint meetings and exchanges of experience, in order to promote the performance and activity of our legislative power, in accordance with current requirements. ladies and gentlemen, my country is facing many challenges at the moment, including a difficult economic environment. we have recently launched an economic plan, the outlines of which outline a road map for the revival of productive sectors and the modernisation of our country's infrastructure. this plan reinforces the decisions taken at the cedre conference. i have made the fight against corruption and the promotion of transparency one of my priorities. we have already made some progress in this area. ladies and gentlemen, lebanon has borne its share of the burden of the economic, social and security crises in the region. terrorist organizations have infiltrated our borders to the east and north to make them a springboard for their bloody operations inside lebanese territory. however, our army launched a successful military offensive, which ended in the defeat of the terrorists and the liberation of our territories. our army has continued its work in collaboration with all state organs and services, in order to dismantle the sleeper terrorist cells until their total elimination. we have thus been able to restore security and stability to the country. the crisis caused by the displaced syrians is the one that weighs heavily on lebanon. the consequences are devastating for the economic, security and social sectors. in the name of human solidarity, lebanon has welcomed over one and a half million refugees who have fled the hell of the war in syria. however, in a small country like ours, with a high population density, limited resources and already suffering from weak infrastructure and rising unemployment, this burden is very difficult to bear. you are well aware of the devastating effects of the presence of syrian refugees. the international community has not taken its share of responsibility in supporting lebanon in the face of the effects of the presence of migrants. we therefore call for the decisions on financial support, in particular those adopted at the brussels conference, to be implemented. we also ask that the reservations we have expressed on certain points in the final communiqué of the brussels conference be taken into account, in particular those relating to the voluntary return of refugees or displaced persons, in particular the link between this return and the political solution, or the integration of migrants or displaced persons into the labour market in the host countries. it should be recalled in this connection that lebanon is a country of emigration and not a country of settlement, much less a labour market. the lebanese people scattered throughout the world, and on the european continent in particular, are proof of this. today, lebanon aims to ensure the safe and dignified return of displaced syrians and rejects any delaying tactics in this regard. my country supports any initiative aimed at finding a solution to the mass displacement of syrians on its territory, as is the case with the russian initiative, and refuses to link the return of displaced people to a political solution that could be delayed. i would like to remind you that since 1948 the palestinian people have been living in camps, particularly in lebanon, awaiting a political solution and the implementation of resolution 194. after 70 years of waiting, instead of a solution to the palestinian problem, a project of permanent settlement is unfortunately beginning to loom on the horizon, as if the international community were adopting the policy of "giving what you do not have to those who do not deserve it". ladies and gentlemen, on this day of 11 september, we commemorate the events of 11 september 2001, a pivotal date that changed the course of world history and led the united states to declare war on terrorism, with the promise of establishing freedom, democracy and putting an end to terrorism. unfortunately, for 17 years now, the war on terrorism has spread terrorism around the world, rather than stopping it. i quote from an open letter i sent to world leaders in 1995, in which i warned them against the danger of the rise of fundamentalism which was beginning to emerge in some countries. i wrote: 'the recent events in bosnia-herzegovina, algeria and, to a lesser extent, paris are only the first signs of the conflicts that will threaten us tomorrow, and which neither nuclear deterrence nor conventional warfare can stop. fundamentalism, born of inequality in the application of international law and of the frustrations of unbalanced development, risks drowning an entire civilization in obscurantism. no one is immune to the violence that ethnic and fundamentalist passions unleash, and no borders can contain it. these viruses, cultivated in the laboratories of political realism, are they not likely to infect those who, indifferently or as accomplices, have allowed them to germinate? unfortunately, the whole world is now suffering the consequences of the experiments of the laboratories of political realism and the ignoring of the warning signs that could have put us on the right track. i would add that international policy in the middle east only exacerbates radicalisation and hatred and opens the way to violence and terrorism. there is no doubt that these unjust policies are throwing a wrench in the implementation of democracy, and this in the pioneer countries, those who were the first to build it into a political system. can preserving human rights at home and sacrificing them abroad help serve the interests of the great powers and be synonymous with democracy? this same policy has led israel to judaize jerusalem and make it its capital, ignoring all international resolutions and the votes of the security council and the un general assembly. israel did not stop there, it even passed the law of the "jewish nation-state". in addition, the recent us decision to stop funding unrwa has marked the beginning of the de facto settlement of palestinians in the countries that have hosted them, including lebanon. an implantation we reject in the name of justice and equality. ladies and gentlemen, i come here today on behalf of lebanon, the 'message country' as the holy father john paul ii called it. its 5,000-year-old civilization, with its deep and multiple roots, has allowed its children, in its many and varied facets and dimensions, to spread across the world and to integrate into all cultures, wherever they go. i take the opportunity to be here today to renew the call, already made, particularly from the united nations platform, to make lebanon a permanent centre of dialogue between civilizations, religions and races. this project is being followed by our ambassadors around the world and we hope that it will be proposed at the next session of the un general assembly in 2019, as we hope that it will receive the support of european countries. who better than the countries of europe to know the importance of dialogue in order to fill the gaps, heal the wounds and establish a culture of peace? i want to thank you for having me here today. thank you for your attention. long live europe, long live lebanon, and long live the people of lebanon.
